1: """Demand distribution shift evaluation suite.
2: 
3: Freezes trained policies and evaluates degradation across shift scenarios
4: defined in docs/demand-shift.md.
5: 
6: Usage:
7:     # Evaluate specific checkpoint:
8:     uv run python -m marl_fleet_rebalancing.experiments.shift_eval \\
9:         --checkpoint runs/mappo-nyc-0916-192802/checkpoint.pt \\
10:         --include-baselines \\
11:         --episodes 5
12: 
13:     # Evaluate all available checkpoints in runs/ and baselines:
14:     uv run python -m marl_fleet_rebalancing.experiments.shift_eval \\
15:         --all-runs \\
16:         --include-baselines
17: 
18: Outputs:
19:     results/shift_eval/summary.json          — aggregated results by policy × shift
20:     results/shift_eval/{policy}.csv          — per-episode KPI traces
21:     results/shift_eval/degradation_table.md  — markdown summary table
22: """
23: 
24: from __future__ import annotations
25: 
26: import argparse
27: import json
28: import sys
29: from pathlib import Path
30: from typing import Any
31: 
32: import numpy as np
33: 
34: sys.path.insert(0, str(Path(__file__).resolve().parents[2]))
35: 
36: from marl_fleet_rebalancing.baselines import GreedyZoneDispatcher, ZoneLPRebalancer
37: from marl_fleet_rebalancing.config import NYCConfig
38: from marl_fleet_rebalancing.env import RideGymAEC
39: from marl_fleet_rebalancing.experiments.evaluate import load_trained_policy
40: from marl_fleet_rebalancing.networks.encoder import obs_to_vector
41: from marl_fleet_rebalancing.shifts import SHIFTS, make_shifted_env
42: 
43: RESULTS_DIR = Path("results/shift_eval")
44: EVAL_EPISODES = 5
45: BASE_SEED = 1000
46: 
47: 
48: def evaluate_policy_on_env(
49:     policy: Any,
50:     env: RideGymAEC,
51:     policy_type: str = "checkpoint",
52:     num_episodes: int = 1,
53:     base_seed: int = BASE_SEED,
54:     deterministic: bool = True,
55: ) -> list[dict[str, float]]:
56:     """Run evaluation episodes over fresh seeds. Returns list of KPI dicts."""
57:     episode_results: list[dict[str, float]] = []
58: 
59:     for ep in range(num_episodes):
60:         seed = base_seed + ep
61:         env.reset(seed=seed)
62:         if hasattr(policy, "reset"):
63:             policy.reset(seed=seed)
64: 
65:         acts_idle = acts_dispatch = acts_relocate = 0
66:         total_reward = 0.0
67: 
68:         while env.agents and not env._terminated:
69:             order = [int(z) for z in env._agent_order]
70:             if env.terminations.get(env.agent_selection, False):
71:                 total_reward += float(sum(
72:                     env.rewards.get(f"zone_{slot}", 0.0) for slot in order
73:                 ))
74:                 for _ in order:
75:                     if not env.agents:
76:                         break
77:                     env.step(None)
78:                 continue
79: 
80:             total_reward += float(sum(
81:                 env.rewards.get(f"zone_{slot}", 0.0) for slot in order
82:             ))
83: 
84:             if policy_type == "checkpoint":
85:                 obs_list = []
86:                 mask_list = []
87:                 for slot in order:
88:                     agent = f"zone_{slot}"
89:                     obs = env.observe(agent)
90:                     obs_list.append(obs_to_vector(obs))
91:                     mask_list.append(env.action_mask(agent))
92: 
93:                 actions, _, _ = policy.act(
94:                     np.stack(obs_list), np.stack(mask_list), deterministic=deterministic,
95:                 )
96:                 for slot, act_v in zip(order, actions):
97:                     act_val = int(act_v)
98:                     if act_val == 0:
99:                         acts_idle += 1
100:                     elif 1 <= act_val <= env._max_pending:
101:                         acts_dispatch += 1
102:                     else:
103:                         acts_relocate += 1
104:                     env.step(act_val)
105: 
106:             elif policy_type in ("greedy", "lp"):
107:                 for slot in order:
108:                     agent = f"zone_{slot}"
109:                     obs = env.observe(agent)
110:                     act_val = policy.select_action(agent, env, obs)
111:                     if act_val == 0:
112:                         acts_idle += 1
113:                     elif 1 <= act_val <= env._max_pending:
114:                         acts_dispatch += 1
115:                     else:
116:                         acts_relocate += 1
117:                     env.step(act_val)
118: 
119:             elif policy_type == "random":
120:                 for slot in order:
121:                     agent = f"zone_{slot}"
122:                     mask = env.action_mask(agent)
123:                     valid_acts = np.where(mask)[0]
124:                     act_val = int(np.random.choice(valid_acts)) if len(valid_acts) > 0 else 0
125:                     if act_val == 0:
126:                         acts_idle += 1
127:                     elif 1 <= act_val <= env._max_pending:
128:                         acts_dispatch += 1
129:                     else:
130:                         acts_relocate += 1
131:                     env.step(act_val)
132:             else:
133:                 raise ValueError(f"Unknown policy_type: {policy_type}")
134: 
135:         kpi = env.kpi_summary()
136:         sr = float(kpi["served"]) / max(float(kpi["served"]) + float(kpi["cancelled"]), 1.0)
137:         total_acts = max(acts_idle + acts_dispatch + acts_relocate, 1)
138: 
139:         episode_results.append({
140:             "episode": float(ep + 1),
141:             "seed": float(seed),
142:             "served": float(kpi["served"]),
143:             "cancelled": float(kpi["cancelled"]),
144:             "service_rate": sr,
145:             "avg_wait_min": float(kpi["avg_wait"]),
146:             "empty_km": float(kpi["empty_km"]),
147:             "total_reward": total_reward,
148:             "pct_dispatch": float(100.0 * acts_dispatch / total_acts),
149:             "pct_relocate": float(100.0 * acts_relocate / total_acts),
150:             "pct_idle": float(100.0 * acts_idle / total_acts),
151:         })
152: 
153:     return episode_results
154: 
155: 
156: def evaluate_policy_across_shifts(
157:     policy_name: str,
158:     policy: Any,
159:     policy_type: str,
160:     base_cfg: NYCConfig | None,
161:     shifts: list[tuple],
162:     num_episodes: int = EVAL_EPISODES,
163:     base_seed: int = BASE_SEED,
164:     deterministic: bool = True,
165: ) -> dict[str, Any]:
166:     """Evaluate a single policy across all shift scenarios."""
167:     print(f"\n{'='*70}")
168:     print(f"Evaluating Policy: {policy_name} ({policy_type})")
169:     print(f"{'='*70}")
170: 
171:     results: dict[str, Any] = {}
172: 
173:     for shift_label, w_limit, t_zone, t_pct, shift_param, desc in shifts:
174:         shift_key = shift_label
175:         print(f"  [{shift_label:<4}] {desc:<45} (limit={w_limit})...", end="", flush=True)
176: 
177:         all_episode_results: list[dict[str, float]] = []
178: 
179:         for seed_idx in range(num_episodes):
180:             env = make_shifted_env(
181:                 shift_label,
182:                 w_limit,
183:                 t_zone,
184:                 t_pct,
185:                 shift_param=shift_param,
186:                 seed=base_seed + seed_idx,
187:                 base_cfg=base_cfg,
188:             )
189:             ep_results = evaluate_policy_on_env(
190:                 policy=policy,
191:                 env=env,
192:                 policy_type=policy_type,
193:                 num_episodes=1,
194:                 base_seed=base_seed + seed_idx,
195:                 deterministic=deterministic,
196:             )
197:             all_episode_results.extend(ep_results)
198:             del env
199: 
200:         # Aggregate episode results
201:         if all_episode_results:
202:             avg: dict[str, Any] = {}
203:             for k in all_episode_results[0]:
204:                 vals = [r[k] for r in all_episode_results]
205:                 avg[k] = {
206:                     "mean": round(float(np.mean(vals)), 6),
207:                     "std": round(float(np.std(vals)), 6),
208:                     "min": round(float(np.min(vals)), 6),
209:                     "max": round(float(np.max(vals)), 6),
210:                 }
211:             avg["episodes"] = len(all_episode_results)
212:             results[shift_key] = avg
213: 
214:             sr = avg.get("service_rate", {}).get("mean", 0)
215:             sr_std = avg.get("service_rate", {}).get("std", 0)
216:             empty = avg.get("empty_km", {}).get("mean", 0)
217:             wait = avg.get("avg_wait_min", {}).get("mean", 0)
218:             print(f" -> SR={sr*100:5.1f}% ±{sr_std*100:4.1f}% | Wait={wait:4.2f}m | Empty={empty:5.0f}km")
219: 
220:     return results
221: 
222: 
223: def format_markdown_table(all_results: dict[str, dict[str, Any]]) -> str:
224:     """Generate a clean markdown summary table comparing policies across shifts."""
225:     lines = []
226:     lines.append("### Demand Shift Degradation Summary (Service Rate % ± Std)")
227:     lines.append("")
228:     header = "| Policy | " + " | ".join([s[0] for s in SHIFTS]) + " | Mean Retention |"
229:     sep = "|:---| " + " | ".join([":---:" for _ in SHIFTS]) + " | :---: |"
230:     lines.append(header)
231:     lines.append(sep)
232: 
233:     for policy_name, policy_res in all_results.items():
234:         s0_sr = policy_res.get("S0", {}).get("service_rate", {}).get("mean", None)
235:         retention_list = []
236:         row_items = [f"**{policy_name}**"]
237: 
238:         for s_label, _, _, _, _, _ in SHIFTS:
239:             s_data = policy_res.get(s_label, {}).get("service_rate", {})
240:             sr_mean = s_data.get("mean", None)
241:             sr_std = s_data.get("std", 0.0)
242:             if sr_mean is not None:
243:                 row_items.append(f"{sr_mean*100:.1f}% ± {sr_std*100:.1f}%")
244:                 if s0_sr and s0_sr > 0 and s_label != "S0":
245:                     retention_list.append(sr_mean / s0_sr)
246:             else:
247:                 row_items.append("N/A")
248: 
249:         mean_ret = f"{np.mean(retention_list)*100:.1f}%" if retention_list else "N/A"
250:         row_items.append(mean_ret)
251:         lines.append("| " + " | ".join(row_items) + " |")
252: 
253:     lines.append("")
254:     lines.append("### Empty Repositioning Distance (km / episode)")
255:     lines.append("")
256:     header = "| Policy | " + " | ".join([s[0] for s in SHIFTS]) + " |"
257:     sep = "|:---| " + " | ".join([":---:" for _ in SHIFTS]) + " |"
258:     lines.append(header)
259:     lines.append(sep)
260: 
261:     for policy_name, policy_res in all_results.items():
262:         row_items = [f"**{policy_name}**"]
263:         for s_label, _, _, _, _, _ in SHIFTS:
264:             e_mean = policy_res.get(s_label, {}).get("empty_km", {}).get("mean", None)
265:             if e_mean is not None:
266:                 row_items.append(f"{e_mean:.0f} km")
267:             else:
268:                 row_items.append("N/A")
269:         lines.append("| " + " | ".join(row_items) + " |")
270: 
271:     lines.append("")
272:     lines.append("### Average Passenger Waiting Time (min)")
273:     lines.append("")
274:     header = "| Policy | " + " | ".join([s[0] for s in SHIFTS]) + " |"
275:     sep = "|:---| " + " | ".join([":---:" for _ in SHIFTS]) + " |"
276:     lines.append(header)
277:     lines.append(sep)
278: 
279:     for policy_name, policy_res in all_results.items():
280:         row_items = [f"**{policy_name}**"]
281:         for s_label, _, _, _, _, _ in SHIFTS:
282:             w_mean = policy_res.get(s_label, {}).get("avg_wait_min", {}).get("mean", None)
283:             if w_mean is not None:
284:                 row_items.append(f"{w_mean:.2f} min")
285:             else:
286:                 row_items.append("N/A")
287:         lines.append("| " + " | ".join(row_items) + " |")
288: 
289:     return "\n".join(lines)
290: 
291: 
292: def main() -> None:
293:     parser = argparse.ArgumentParser(description="Evaluate policies across demand shift suite.")
294:     parser.add_argument(
295:         "--checkpoint",
296:         type=str,
297:         default=None,
298:         help="Path to specific model checkpoint (e.g. runs/mappo-nyc-.../checkpoint.pt)",
299:     )
300:     parser.add_argument(
301:         "--all-runs",
302:         action="store_true",
303:         help="Discover and evaluate all checkpoints found in runs/",
304:     )
305:     parser.add_argument(
306:         "--include-baselines",
307:         action="store_true",
308:         help="Also evaluate non-RL baselines (Greedy & LP)",
309:     )
310:     parser.add_argument(
311:         "--baselines-only",
312:         action="store_true",
313:         help="Only evaluate non-RL baselines (Greedy & LP)",
314:     )
315:     parser.add_argument(
316:         "--episodes",
317:         type=int,
318:         default=EVAL_EPISODES,
319:         help="Number of evaluation seeds per scenario (default: 5)",
320:     )
321:     parser.add_argument(
322:         "--base-seed",
323:         type=int,
324:         default=BASE_SEED,
325:         help="Base seed for evaluation (default: 1000)",
326:     )
327:     parser.add_argument(
328:         "--stochastic",
329:         action="store_true",
330:         help="Use stochastic sampling instead of deterministic argmax",
331:     )
332:     parser.add_argument(
333:         "--out-dir",
334:         type=str,
335:         default=str(RESULTS_DIR),
336:         help="Output directory for results",
337:     )
338:     args = parser.parse_args()
339: 
340:     out_path = Path(args.out_dir)
341:     out_path.mkdir(parents=True, exist_ok=True)
342: 
343:     policies_to_evaluate = []
344: 
345:     # 1. Checkpoint discovery
346:     if args.checkpoint:
347:         ckpt_p = Path(args.checkpoint)
348:         if not ckpt_p.exists():
349:             raise FileNotFoundError(f"Checkpoint not found: {ckpt_p}")
350:         p_name = ckpt_p.parent.name
351:         policies_to_evaluate.append((p_name, "checkpoint", ckpt_p))
352:     elif args.all_runs:
353:         runs_dir = Path("runs")
354:         if runs_dir.exists():
355:             for p in sorted(runs_dir.iterdir()):
356:                 ckpt = p / "checkpoint.pt"
357:                 if ckpt.exists():
358:                     policies_to_evaluate.append((p.name, "checkpoint", ckpt))
359:     elif not args.baselines_only:
360:         # Default: pick latest run in runs/
361:         runs_dir = Path("runs")
362:         found = False
363:         if runs_dir.exists():
364:             runs = sorted(runs_dir.iterdir(), key=lambda p: p.stat().st_mtime, reverse=True)
365:             for r in runs:
366:                 ckpt = r / "checkpoint.pt"
367:                 if ckpt.exists():
368:                     policies_to_evaluate.append((r.name, "checkpoint", ckpt))
369:                     found = True
370:                     break
371:         if not found:
372:             print("No checkpoints found. Falling back to baselines.")
373:             args.include_baselines = True
374: 
375:     # 2. Baselines
376:     if args.include_baselines or args.baselines_only:
377:         policies_to_evaluate.append(("Greedy-Baseline", "greedy", None))
378:         policies_to_evaluate.append(("LP-Rebalancer", "lp", None))
379: 
380:     all_results: dict[str, dict[str, Any]] = {}
381: 
382:     for policy_name, p_type, ckpt_or_none in policies_to_evaluate:
383:         if p_type == "checkpoint":
384:             learner, _manifest, eval_env = load_trained_policy(Path(ckpt_or_none))
385:             base_cfg = eval_env._cfg
386:             res = evaluate_policy_across_shifts(
387:                 policy_name=policy_name,
388:                 policy=learner,
389:                 policy_type="checkpoint",
390:                 base_cfg=base_cfg,
391:                 shifts=SHIFTS,
392:                 num_episodes=args.episodes,
393:                 base_seed=args.base_seed,
394:                 deterministic=not args.stochastic,
395:             )
396:         elif p_type == "greedy":
397:             policy = GreedyZoneDispatcher()
398:             base_cfg = NYCConfig()
399:             res = evaluate_policy_across_shifts(
400:                 policy_name=policy_name,
401:                 policy=policy,
402:                 policy_type="greedy",
403:                 base_cfg=base_cfg,
404:                 shifts=SHIFTS,
405:                 num_episodes=args.episodes,
406:                 base_seed=args.base_seed,
407:                 deterministic=True,
408:             )
409:         elif p_type == "lp":
410:             policy = ZoneLPRebalancer()
411:             base_cfg = NYCConfig()
412:             res = evaluate_policy_across_shifts(
413:                 policy_name=policy_name,
414:                 policy=policy,
415:                 policy_type="lp",
416:                 base_cfg=base_cfg,
417:                 shifts=SHIFTS,
418:                 num_episodes=args.episodes,
419:                 base_seed=args.base_seed,
420:                 deterministic=True,
421:             )
422:         elif p_type == "random":
423:             policy = None
424:             base_cfg = NYCConfig()
425:             res = evaluate_policy_across_shifts(
426:                 policy_name=policy_name,
427:                 policy=policy,
428:                 policy_type="random",
429:                 base_cfg=base_cfg,
430:                 shifts=SHIFTS,
431:                 num_episodes=args.episodes,
432:                 base_seed=args.base_seed,
433:                 deterministic=True,
434:             )
435:         else:
436:             continue
437: 
438:         all_results[policy_name] = res
439: 
440:         # Write per-policy CSV
441:         csv_file = out_path / f"{policy_name}.csv"
442:         with open(csv_file, "w", encoding="utf-8") as f:
443:             f.write("shift,metric,mean,std,min,max\n")
444:             for shift_k, s_data in res.items():
445:                 for m_k, m_vals in s_data.items():
446:                     if isinstance(m_vals, dict) and "mean" in m_vals:
447:                         f.write(
448:                             f"{shift_k},{m_k},{m_vals['mean']},{m_vals['std']},"
449:                             f"{m_vals['min']},{m_vals['max']}\n"
450:                         )
451: 
452:     # Save summary JSON
453:     summary_path = out_path / "summary.json"
454:     with open(summary_path, "w", encoding="utf-8") as f:
455:         json.dump(all_results, f, indent=2)
456:     print(f"\n[Saved JSON summary to {summary_path}]")
457: 
458:     # Generate Markdown table
459:     md_content = format_markdown_table(all_results)
460:     md_path = out_path / "degradation_table.md"
461:     with open(md_path, "w", encoding="utf-8") as f:
462:         f.write(md_content)
463:     print(f"[Saved Markdown report to {md_path}]")
464: 
465:     # Print summary table to stdout
466:     print("\n" + md_content + "\n")
467: 
468: 
469: if __name__ == "__main__":
470:     main()

1: """Central configuration for all experiment parameters.
2: 
3: Every numeric constant lives here — no magic numbers in env or training code.
4: NYC defaults are documented with their source/rationale.
5: 
6: Usage:
7:     from marl_fleet_rebalancing.config import NYCConfig
8:     cfg = NYCConfig()
9:     env = RideGymAEC(cfg)
10: """
11: 
12: from __future__ import annotations
13: 
14: from dataclasses import dataclass, field
15: from typing import Any
16: 
17: 
18: @dataclass
19: class EnvConfig:
20:     """Environment configuration shared across all experiments.
21: 
22:     Version: 1.9 (D28: relocation arrival bonus & NYC action-space compression)
23:     """
24: 
25:     # --- Fleet ---
26:     num_drivers: int = 50
27:     driver_capacity: int = 4
28: 
29:     # --- Dispatch & Relocation policies (D14, D15) ---
30:     # When True, zone agents can opportunistically dispatch orders to empty
31:     # drivers in status RELOCATING in addition to IDLE drivers.
32:     allow_relocating_dispatch: bool = True
33:     # Maximum distance (km) from zone centroid for an order to be considered a
34:     # candidate for dispatch. Prevents cross-city deadheading (D15). None disables.
35:     max_dispatch_radius_km: float | None = 2.0
36:     # When True, relocation actions are restricted to adjacent zone neighbours (D15, D26).
37:     # Creates smooth local spatial diffusion without long-distance highway locks.
38:     neighbor_relocation_only: bool = True
39: 
40:     # --- Parameterized Zone Demand (D15) ---
41:     # Specific zone ID (0 .. num_zones - 1) to receive a dedicated share of demand.
42:     target_zone: int | None = None
43:     # Percentage (0.0 to 100.0) of total episode demand originating in target_zone.
44:     target_zone_demand_pct: float | None = None
45: 
46:     # --- Demand-shift mechanisms (D21; eval-side scenario knobs) ---
47:     # Exactly one of "none" | "burst" | "permute" applies per scenario;
48:     # "concentrate" is expressed with target_zone/target_zone_demand_pct
49:     # above (S4 cold zones). All preserve order count and timestamps.
50:     demand_shift_mode: str = "none"
51:     # S5 event burst: share (0..100) of orders with request_time in
52:     # [burst_start_min, burst_end_min) reassigned to burst_zone.
53:     burst_zone: int | None = None
54:     burst_start_min: float = 20.0
55:     burst_end_min: float = 30.0
56:     burst_share_pct: float | None = None
57:     # S1 spatial permutation: per-order remap probability (0..1) through a
58:     # fixed rotation of offset perm_offset zones (None = num_zones // 2).
59:     perm_strength: float | None = None
60:     perm_offset: int | None = None
61: 
62:     # --- Simulation ---
63:     dt: float = 0.5             # minutes per step (30 sim-seconds, D23)
64:     horizon: float = 60.0       # episode length in minutes
65:     order_timeout: float = 2.0  # minutes before unassigned order is cancelled
66: 
67:     # --- Order generation ---
68:     num_orders: int = 800       # total orders to generate over the episode
69:     max_party_size: int = 3
70: 
71:     # --- Network ---
72:     network_path: str = "data/nyc/manhattan.gpickle"
73:     speed_kmh: float = 30.0
74: 
75:     # --- Observation budget ---
76:     # Maximum pending orders included in observation.
77:     # If actual pending > max_pending, we keep the most urgent orders
78:     # (closest to timeout) and mask the rest.
79:     # Bandra West measured max ~70; NYC measured peak ~28 concurrent (D28).
80:     # NYCConfig overrides to 30; BandraConfig keeps 80.
81:     max_pending: int = 80
82: 
83:     # --- Region model (zone-controller agents, D10) ---
84:     # Synthetic maps: rows x cols grid of zone centres over the network bounds
85:     # (Bandra default 5x5 = 25 zones). NYC overrides this with real taxi-zone
86:     # centroids (nyc_zone_centroids_path).
87:     relocation_grid: tuple[int, int] = (5, 5)
88: 
89:     # --- Order source ---
90:     # "synthetic": OSMnxOrderGenerator over the network (num_orders, max_party_size)
91:     # "nyc":       NYC FHVHV splits via MultiWindowNYCOrderGenerator — one
92:     #              window per episode (train: random draw; val/test: cyclic);
93:     #              endpoints are zone centroids snapped to network nodes.
94:     #              See docs/nyc-pipeline.md.
95:     # "dataframe": raw DataFrameOrderGenerator — endpoints must already lie on
96:     #              the network (no snapping is performed).
97:     order_source: str = "synthetic"
98:     dataframe: Any = None                          # only for order_source="dataframe"
99:     dataframe_columns: dict[str, str] | None = None
100: 
101:     # --- NYC split parameters (order_source="nyc") ---
102:     nyc_split: str = "train"                       # "train" | "val" | "test"
103:     nyc_splits_dir: str = "data/nyc/splits"
104:     nyc_zone_centroids_path: str = "data/nyc/zone_centroids.csv"
105:     # Per-window cap on orders (sample-rate lever; None = full historical rate).
106:     # Open experiment-design decision (HANDOFF): full-rate peaks (~15k trips/hr)
107:     # are unservable by a 50-driver fleet. MUST be settled before training.
108:     nyc_window_limit: int | None = None
109:     nyc_random_party_size: bool = False  # FHVHV carries no real party size
110:     nyc_max_party_size: int = 3
111:     # Spatial jitter (km) of zone-centroid endpoints before snapping; mitigates
112:     # the centroid-collapse artefact. 0 keeps exact centroids.
113:     nyc_perturb_od_radius_km: float = 0.0
114: 
115:     # --- Reward normalization ---
116:     # Empty-distance normalizer in km: the beta term applies per
117:     # (empty km traveled / this value). Deadhead distance is taken exactly from
118:     # the RideGym step event log (D13).
119:     empty_distance_norm_km: float = 1.0
120: 
121:     # --- Reward weights ---
122:     # R = -alpha * waiting_time - beta * empty_distance + gamma * served_demand - cancel_penalty
123:     # Scaled cleanly so serving (+1.0) balances cancellation (-1.0) and empty distance (-0.15/km, D25).
124:     alpha: float = 0.2   # weight for passenger waiting time (D25)
125:     beta: float = 0.15   # weight for empty repositioning distance (cost per empty km, D25)
126:     gamma: float = 1.0   # weight for served demand (payoff per completed order, D25)
127:     cancel_penalty: float = 1.0  # penalty per cancelled order in zone (D25)
128:     team_share: float = 0.2  # 80% local credit + 20% team
129: 
130:     # --- Relocation arrival bonus (D28) ---
131:     # When a relocated driver arrives at its destination zone, the relocator zone
132:     # receives an immediate bonus: prospective_bonus_scale * pending_demand_at_dest / num_drivers.
133:     # Eliminates the 8-18 turn credit delay that made relocation perpetually unprofitable.
134:     # 0.0 disables (legacy behavior). Typical: 1.0-3.0.
135:     prospective_bonus_scale: float = 0.0
136: 
137:     # --- Seeding ---
138:     seed: int | None = None
139: 
140: 
141: # ---------------------------------------------------------------------------
142: # NYC-specific preset
143: # ---------------------------------------------------------------------------
144: 
145: @dataclass
146: class NYCConfig(EnvConfig):
147:     """Configuration for NYC Manhattan study area.
148:

1: # Batch 2 — MARL for Dynamic Fleet Rebalancing
2: 
3: **Project:** Multi-Agent Reinforcement Learning for Dynamic Fleet Rebalancing under Uncertain Demand
4: 
5: **Stack:** Python + SUMO (or a custom Gymnasium environment)
6: **Graded on:** Difficulty · Impact · Recency bias (future relevance)
7: 
8: ---
9: 
10: ## Scorecard
11: 
12: | Dimension | Score | Verdict |
13: |---|---|---|
14: | **Difficulty** | 4.5 / 5 | Steepest of the four; MARL + environment engineering + robustness eval |
15: | **Impact** | 4.5 / 5 | Operational core of ride-hailing; open problem named by surveys |
16: | **Recency bias (future relevance)** | 4 / 5 | RL for fleets is here to stay; pure MARL hype peaked 2021–22 |
17: | **Publishability** | 3.5 / 5 | Workshop level realistic if evaluation is rigorous |
18: 
19: ---
20: 
21: ## 1. Why it is hard (in detail)
22: 
23: MARL inherits every difficulty of single-agent RL and adds two structural problems that break RL's theoretical guarantees:
24: 
25: 1. **Non-stationarity.** Each agent's environment changes as other agents update their policies, violating the stationarity assumption that underpins value learning. During training you will see reward spikes and collapses. The IPPO paper argues independent learning works *because* it is robust to some of this.
26: 2. **Credit assignment.** With a shared fleet reward, individual zone-controllers can't tell whose action contributed to the outcome → "lazy agents" and relative overgeneralization.
27: 3. **Hyperparameter sensitivity.** The MAPPO paper documents severe sensitivity (15 PPO epochs on hard maps produces suboptimal policies vs 5–10). There is **no convergence guarantee** in general MARL; results are seed-sensitive. The RL Baselines3 Zoo docs exist precisely because defaults fail.
28: 4. **Reward shaping.** `R = −α·waiting − β·empty_km + γ·served_demand` — mismatched α/β/γ scales collapse learning onto one objective. This is a notorious, time-eating pain point.
29: 5. **Long training times.** On-policy PPO is sample-inefficient; MAPPO needs large batches and ~10⁵–10⁶ environment steps, each step a simulation tick.
30: 6. **The whole thesis is a robustness study.** "Train under demand D, evaluate under D', peak, event" — RL agents overfit to training dynamics, so you must build a rigorous eval harness (normal/shifted/peak/event), not just a learning curve. This is well-aligned with the research question but doubles the experimental surface.
31: 
32: **The SUMO question (huge difficulty lever).** SUMO's learning curve is genuinely steep: XML network/route files, `netedit`, the TraCI client/server loop, demand calibration. The `sumo-rl` wrapper exists but is built for traffic-signal control, not zone rebalancing. **Honest recommendation:** for 5–10 zones and 10–30 vehicles, build a **custom Gymnasium environment** — dramatically simpler, faster, and more controllable than SUMO, and the project brief explicitly permits it. Skipping SUMO lowers difficulty significantly. If SUMO is used, prefer `libsumo` (embedded) over the socket-based TraCI for speed.
33: 
34: **Skills required:** MDP/Dec-POMDP and CTDE theory, PPO mechanics (clipped surrogate objective, GAE, observation normalization), MAPPO vs IPPO distinction, Gymnasium API, Stable-Baselines3 or RLlib (steep) or hand-rolled IPPO/MAPPO in PyTorch (~300–800 lines), reward shaping, ≥5 random seeds + confidence intervals.
35: 
36: **Learning-curve verdict:** the steepest of the four projects. It stacks RL fundamentals + multi-agent theory + environment engineering + a robustness-evaluation protocol. The "zone controllers as agents" simplification is a sensible de-risking step, but the core MARL pathologies remain.
37: 
38: ### Sources
39: - Yu et al., *The Surprising Effectiveness of PPO in Cooperative Multi-Agent Games* (MAPPO) — https://arxiv.org/abs/2103.01955
40: - Schroeder de Witt et al., *Is Independent Learning All You Need?* (IPPO) — https://arxiv.org/abs/2011.09533
41: - Huh & Mohapatra, *Multi-agent Reinforcement Learning: A Comprehensive Survey* — https://arxiv.org/abs/2312.10256
42: - Donatus et al., *Multi-Agent RL in Intelligent Transportation Systems: A Comprehensive Survey* (names scalability, non-stationarity, sim-to-real as key barriers) — https://arxiv.org/abs/2508.20315
43: - Fujimoto et al., *Assessing the Impact of Distribution Shift on RL Performance* (methodological template for your eval harness) — https://arxiv.org/abs/2402.03590
44: - Lin et al., *Efficient Large-Scale Fleet Management via Multi-Agent Deep RL* (DiDi) — https://arxiv.org/abs/1802.06444
45: - Qin et al., *Ride-Hailing Order Dispatching at DiDi via RL* (industry deployment proof) — https://doi.org/10.1287/inte.2020.1047
46: - Tang et al., *Value Function is All You Need* (V1D3) — https://arxiv.org/abs/2105.08791
47: - SUMO / TraCI docs — https://sumo.dlr.de/docs/TraCI/Interfacing_TraCI_from_Python.html
48: - Stable-Baselines3 — https://stable-baselines3.readthedocs.io/ · RL Zoo tuning — https://rl-baselines3-zoo.readthedocs.io/en/master/guide/tuning.html
49: - RLlib — https://docs.ray.io/en/latest/rllib/index.html · SUMO-RL wrapper — https://github.com/LucasAlegre/sumo-rl
50: 
51: ---
52: 
53: ## 2. Impact
54: 
55: **Who cares:** Uber, Lyft, Didi (DiDi publicly shipped RL-based dispatching/repositioning to production — tens of millions of daily orders; it hosted the KDD Cup 2020 "Learn to Dispatch" RL track), Waymo/Zoox robo-taxis, microtransit operators. The topic is actively worked on by both the AI community (KDD, NeurIPS, AAMAS) and the transportation-science community (IEEE TITS, TRC, ITSC).
56: 
57: **Publishability — this is the project's strongest asset.** Distribution shift / sim-to-real / demand uncertainty is repeatedly named an *open problem* (the 2025 ITS-MARL survey calls sim-to-real transfer a "major barrier to practical deployment"). A clean empirical study that (i) builds a reproducible zone-level rebalancing benchmark, (ii) trains IPPO/MAPPO under one demand distribution, and (iii) measures degradation under normal/shifted/peak/event demand vs greedy and demand-heuristic baselines is a genuine citable contribution — workshop paper (NeurIPS/ICML RL workshops, AAMAS, ITSC) or transport journal if the analysis is rigorous.
58: 
59: **Caveats:** (a) "train-then-test-under-shift" without any mitigation (domain randomization, offline RL, adaptation) is a common framing — your contribution must come from transport-specific depth (multi-zone heterogeneity, event surges, fleet-size sensitivity) and solid statistics; (b) a negative result ("advantage degrades") is scientifically valuable but harder to publish alone — pair it with *why* (which reward component collapses) and with a mitigation baseline.
60: 
61: ---
62: 
63: ## 3. Recency bias — will it be "used properly" in the future?
64: 
65: **Not stagnating — but be precise about where.** The literature is expanding (comprehensive MARL-in-ITS survey Aug 2025; new papers through 2026 on MARL dispatching, hierarchical fleet management, competitive multi-operator rebalancing). Skill transfer is strong: PPO, actor-critic, reward shaping, environment engineering, and OOD evaluation transfer directly to robotics (sim-to-real), recommender systems, supply chain/ops, and autoscaling.
66: 
67: **The honest caution:** the *hype* peaked ~2021–2022 (MAPPO/IPPO fame); attention has shifted to LLM-based agents and foundation models. More importantly, **the largest production successes (DiDi dispatch, V1D3, KDD Cup winners) are single-agent value-based RL plus planning — not distributed MARL.** Fully independent MARL agents in production are rare; real fleets are usually controlled by one learned value function dispatching many vehicles.
68: 
69: **Implication for your framing:** "MARL as the architecture" may age worse than "RL for fleet control," which is clearly here to stay. So frame the contribution around **robustness to demand shift** — a named, unsolved barrier to real deployment. That angle is future-proof whether MARL-the-paradigm or RL-the-tool wins the deployment race.
70: 
71: ---
72: 
73: ## Bottom line
74: 
75: Difficult (steepest if you use SUMO; manageable if you build a custom Gym env and implement IPPO first, MAPPO after), high skill-growth, and the best alignment with the field's most-cited open problem (distribution shift). Realistic publishability at workshop level **provided** the evaluation protocol (seeds, CIs, ablations) is rigorous.

30: ## 1. Project Overview & Research Question
31: 
32: This project is one of four bachelor research projects sharing the umbrella question:
33: **"When does cooperation improve transportation-system performance?"**
34: 
35: PS2 sits at the **Operational** level of the portfolio: *"Can learning agents allocate scarce mobility resources efficiently?"*
36: 
37: **Research question:**
38: > How robust is cooperative MARL when passenger-demand patterns at deployment differ from those used during training?
39: 
40: **Hypothesis (as stated in the brief):**
41: > MARL improves waiting time and fleet utilization over simple dispatch heuristics, but its advantage degrades under strong demand distribution shift.
42: 
43: **Why this is worth doing:** Distribution shift / sim-to-real transfer is repeatedly named an *open problem* in the 2025 ITS-MARL survey ("major barrier to practical deployment"). A clean empirical study measuring degradation is a genuine, citable contribution.
44: 
45: **Important framing note:** You are NOT trying to invent a new algorithm. The brief explicitly says the contribution should be framed around the experimental question. Even a null result (heuristics beat MARL under shift) is publishable if the comparison is fair.
46: 
47: ---
48: 
49: ## 2. Environment Specification (from the brief)
50: 
51: ### Minimal environment
52: - **5–10 zones**, approximately **10–30 vehicles**
53: - **Zone controllers as agents** (NOT individual vehicles) — the brief's explicit simplification to reduce complexity
54: - State: **vehicle distribution, current demand, recent demand**
55: - Actions: **stay** or **reposition vehicles toward neighboring zones**
56: - Reward:
57:   ```
58:   R = − α · waiting time − β · empty distance + γ · served demand
59:   ```
60: 
61: ### Required baselines & experiments
62: | # | Baseline / Method | Notes |
63: |---|---|---|
64: | 1 | Greedy nearest-vehicle dispatch | Simplest floor |
65: | 2 | Demand-based heuristic | Rebalance toward forecast/predicted demand |
66: | 3 | MARL policy (IPPO / MAPPO) | IPPO first; MAPPO if implementation capacity allows |
67: 
68: - **Train under ONE demand distribution**, then evaluate under: **normal, shifted, peak, and event-demand** scenarios.
69: 
70: ### Metrics (from brief)
71: - Passenger waiting time
72: - Service rate
73: - Empty vehicle-km
74: - Fleet utilization
75: - Cumulative reward
76: 
77: ### Platform
78: - **Python** (custom Gymnasium environment) — SUMO is optional. The brief lists "Python / SUMO" as platform; the `02-batch2` deep-dive strongly recommends a custom Gymnasium env for zone-level rebalancing because `sumo-rl` is built for signal control, not rebalancing, and SUMO adds XML/TraCI learning-curve overhead.
79:

89: | **Schroeder de Witt et al. — Is Independent Learning All You Need? (IPPO)** | https://arxiv.org/abs/2011.09533 | IPPO — your first training target; argues independent learning is robust to non-stationarity |
90: | **Lin et al. — Efficient Large-Scale Fleet Management via Multi-Agent Deep RL (DiDi)** | https://arxiv.org/abs/1802.06444 | The canonical MARL fleet-management paper; direct precedent for your project |

94: | Paper | Link | Why you need it |
95: |---|---|---|

97: | **Tang et al. — A Deep Value-network Based Approach for Multi-Driver Order Dispatching (KDD'19)** | https://arxiv.org/abs/2106.04493 · https://dl.acm.org/doi/10.1145/3292500.3330724 | A/B tested live on DiDi; value networks + cross-city transfer |
98: | **Qin et al. — Ride-Hailing Order Dispatching at DiDi via Reinforcement Learning (2020)** | https://doi.org/10.1287/inte.2020.1047 · https://pubsonline.informs.org/doi/10.1287/inte.2020.1047 | Public proof of RL deployed at tens of millions of orders/day |
99: | **Tang et al. — Value Function is All You Need: A Unified Learning Framework for Ride-Hailing Platforms (V1D3, KDD'21)** | https://arxiv.org/abs/2105.08791 | Unified dispatching + repositioning; beat both KDD Cup 2020 winners |
100: | **MOVI: A Model-Free Approach to Dynamic Fleet Management (INFOCOM'18)** | https://www.semanticscholar.org/paper/MOVI%3A-A-Model-Free-Approach-to-Dynamic-Fleet-Oda-Joe-Wong/ (search: "MOVI Oda Joe-Wong") | DQN-based dispatch; argues model-free RL works, "limited value to coordinating vehicle actions" — a counterpoint to MARL you should engage |
101: 
102: **Key insight to use in your intro/related work:** the largest production successes are **single-agent value-based RL + planning**, not fully distributed MARL. Frame your contribution around *RL for fleet control under demand shift*, which is future-proof either way.
103:

168: ## 4. 8-Week Execution Plan with Hard Gates
169: 
170: The brief's Common Execution Plan defines the week-by-week structure and two **mandatory hard gates**.
171: 
172: | Week | Focus | Mandatory deliverable |
173: |---|---|---|
174: | 1 | Literature review; finalize question; install/reproduce environment | **2–3 page related-work map + running simulator** |
175: | 2 | Scenario, metrics, data pipeline | **Simulation produces CSV results** — *HARD GATE 1* |
176: | 3 | Implement first baseline (greedy) | Baseline results and plots |
177: | 4 | Implement proposed MAS/MARL method (IPPO) | **End-to-end comparison runs** — *HARD GATE 2* |
178: | 5 | Run first complete experiment set | Initial result tables |
179: | 6 | Ablation / sensitivity / robustness experiments | Final experimental dataset |
180: | 7 | Statistical analysis, figures, paper drafting | Near-complete manuscript |
181: | 8 | Final runs, paper revision, code cleanup | **Submission-ready paper + repository** |
182: 
183: ### Week-by-week detail
184: 
185: **Week 1 — Foundations**
186: - Read the 4 anchor papers: MAPPO, IPPO, DiDi fleet (Lin 2018), Fujimoto dist-shift.
187: - Read the brief's "Selected Research Foundations": Donatus 2025, Zhang 2024, Rong 2024, Li 2023 (simulator review), Kheterpal 2018 (Flow).
188: - Write the 2–3 page related-work map. Structure: (a) RL fleet management, (b) MARL for transport, (c) robustness/distribution-shift, (d) your gap.
189: - Get *something* running. Recommended: a minimal custom Gymnasium env with a hand-coded greedy policy.
190: - Decision to lock: **SUMO vs custom Gymnasium.** Recommended: custom Gymnasium (see Section 2).
191: 
192: **Week 2 — Environment + data pipeline (HARD GATE 1)**
193: - Build zones, demand generator (poisson arrival per zone per time step, time-of-day profile), vehicle dynamics (travel time between zones), trip origin-destination matrix.
194: - Implement the reward exactly as `R = −α·waiting − β·empty_km + γ·served`. **Freeze α/β/γ values early** and log each term separately.
195: - CSV schema (lock it now): `step, zone_id, arrivals, waiting_time, served, empty_km, utilization, reward_alpha, reward_beta, reward_gamma, total_reward`.
196: - ⚠️ **HARD GATE 1 (mandatory):** simulation must emit machine-readable CSV results by end of this week. Do not proceed until this works.
197: 
198: **Week 3 — Baselines**
199: - Implement **greedy nearest-vehicle dispatch** (assign nearest idle vehicle to each request).
200: - Implement **demand-based heuristic** (reposition idle vehicles toward zones with highest short-horizon forecast demand).
201: - Produce baseline results + plots across all four evaluation regimes (normal/shifted/peak/event) using a *fixed* trained-once-free policy — baselines don't learn.
202: - These two baselines validate the environment and define your floor.
203: 
204: **Week 4 — MARL method (HARD GATE 2)**
205: - Implement **IPPO**: each zone controller runs its own PPO on its own observation with the shared fleet reward. Use Stable-Baselines3 or hand-roll ~300–500 lines of PyTorch.
206: - Only attempt **MAPPO** (centralized critic, CTDE) after IPPO is stable.
207: - ⚠️ **HARD GATE 2 (mandatory):** at least one baseline AND the MARL method must run end-to-end by end of this week.
208: 
209: **Week 5 — First full experiment set**
210: - Train under ONE demand distribution (pick "normal" as training regime).
211: - Evaluate trained policies under: **normal** (iid), **shifted** (different spatial/temporal pattern), **peak** (2–3× surge), **event** (one-off disruption, e.g., a metro shutdown in a zone).
212: - Run **≥5 random seeds** for every condition. Record mean ± 95% CI.
213: - Produce initial result tables: method × regime × metric.
214: 
215: **Week 6 — Ablations & robustness**
216: - **Reward-weight ablation:** re-evaluate with α=0 or β=0 → which reward component collapses under shift? (Diagnoses *why* advantage degrades — this is what makes your paper interesting.)
217: - Fleet-size sensitivity (10 vs 20 vs 30 vehicles); zone heterogeneity; number of seeds convergence.
218: - Optional mitigation baseline: domain-randomized training (train under random demand draws) — this converts a pure negative result into a complete story.
219: - Final experimental dataset delivered.
220: 
221: **Week 7 — Statistics + drafting**
222: - Statistical analysis: CIs, paired tests (e.g., bootstrap or Wilcoxon) between methods per regime.
223: - Figures: learning curves (with CI bands), degradation curves across regimes, per-zone heatmaps.
224: - Draft the paper using the brief's structure (Section 7).
225: 
226: **Week 8 — Finalize**
227: - Re-run final experiments, fix figures, write the repository README.
228: - Code cleanup: seed control, config files (α/β/γ, PPO hyperparameters), `requirements.txt`, reproducibility instructions.
229: - **Submission-ready paper + repository.**
230: 
231: ---

233: ## 5. Team Roles (for a 3–5 person team)
234: 
235: | Role | Responsibility | Suggested count |
236: |---|---|---|
237: | **Environment engineer** | Zones, demand model, vehicles, reward, CSV pipeline, Gymnasium API | 1 |
238: | **Algorithm engineer** | IPPO/MAPPO implementation, training loop, hyperparameter tuning | 1–2 |
239: | **Evaluation / statistics lead** | Seeds, CIs, significance tests, eval harness (normal/shifted/peak/event), ablation runs | 1 |
240: | **Writing / figures** | Paper drafting, related-work map, plots, repository cleanup | 1 (can overlap) |
241:

243: 
244: ## 6. Risk Register & Mitigations
245: 
246: | Risk | Likelihood | Mitigation |
247: |---|---|---|
248: | **MARL pathologies** (non-stationarity, credit assignment, seed sensitivity) | Certain | ≥5 seeds; IPPO before MAPPO; small env (5 zones / 10–20 vehicles) first |
249: | **Reward scale collapse** (one α/β/γ dominates) | High | Freeze weights early; log each term separately; normalize reward components |
250: | **Training too slow** | High | On-policy PPO is sample-hungry (~10⁵–10⁶ steps); vectorize envs (parallel instances); cap episode length |
251: | **Hyperparameter fragility** | High | Follow MAPPO paper guidance (5–10 epochs, not 15); RL Zoo for defaults |
252: | **Evaluation eats the schedule** | High | The shift study IS the contribution — reserve Weeks 5–6, do not compress |
253: | **Silent environment bugs invalidate results** | Medium | Baselines-first validates the env; unit-test demand generator & reward math |
254: | **SUMOV learning-curve trap** | Medium | Use custom Gymnasium env (recommended); skip SUMO entirely |
255: | **Null result (MARL doesn't beat heuristics)** | Possible | By design it's publishable — pair with *why* (reward ablation) and a mitigation baseline |
256:

259: ## 7. Expected Paper Structure (from the brief)
260: 
261: 1. **Introduction** — problem, gap, research question, contributions
262: 2. **Related Work** — focused review, not a broad survey
263: 3. **Methodology** — environment, agents, observations, actions, coordination, algorithm
264: 4. **Experimental Setup** — scenarios, baselines, metrics, seeds, hardware/software
265: 5. **Results** — main comparison PLUS one robustness/ablation study
266: 6. **Discussion** — what worked, what failed, limitations, external validity
267: 7. **Conclusion** — concise answer to the research question
268: 
269: > "The contribution should be framed around the experimental question rather than claiming a new algorithm unless the students genuinely develop one." — program brief

1: # PS2 — What's Still Missing (Explained in Simple Words)
2: 
3: **Project:** MARL for Dynamic Fleet Rebalancing — teaching zone controllers to reposition idle cars to where riders will be.
4: 
5: **The one question:** Does the AI still work well when passenger demand at deployment looks different from the demand it trained on?
6: 
7: ---
8: 
9: ## 1. The 5 gaps (things nobody has done yet)
10: 
11: ### Gap 1 — Nobody ran the exact test you're being asked to run
12: A 2024 paper by **Fujimoto et al.** invented the *right way* to test an AI when things change:
13: > Train it on one pattern → then test it on other patterns → watch how fast it breaks.
14: 
15: But he tested his method on **video games (Atari) and power grids**, **not on cars**.
16: 
17: Papers that DID test ride-hailing rebalancing each miss a piece:
18: - One tested disruptions from **broken train lines** (a supply problem), not demand changes.
19: - One tested with a totally different AI approach (mean-field, for thousands of cars).
20: - One only tested a *fix* (a robustness trick), not the measurement itself.
21: 
22: **So: your exact question is still unanswered.** That's a real, usable gap.
23: 
24: ### Gap 2 — Nobody compared the two AI training styles under change
25: There are two standard ways to train your agents:
26: - **IPPO** — each district manager learns alone.
27: - **MAPPO** — the district managers learn together (shared brain during training) but act alone afterward.
28: 
29: Research shows MAPPO can "memorize" its training data too well — a known issue called **policy overfitting** (a paper by Hu et al. 2021 called it POMAC).
30: 
31: But **nobody has measured: when demand shifts, which one breaks less?**
32: 
33: One-line hypothesis you can test: *MAPPO memorizes training demand more → IPPO survives demand shifts better.*
34: 
35: ### Gap 3 — Everyone compares against "weak" simple baselines
36: "Baselines" = the simple strategies you compare against, e.g.:
37: - Greedy: send idle cars to the busiest zone.
38: - Demand-based: send cars to zones where demand is predicted next.
39: 
40: A very recent paper (**RLSCALE-BENCH**, 2026, on cloud-server autoscaling) found something shocking:
41: > When you **tune the simple baseline properly**, it beats ALL six fancy AI methods — and the winner completely changes when conditions change.
42: 
43: Fleet-management papers almost never tune their baselines, so "AI beats greedy" might just mean "the greedy baseline was bad."
44: 
45: **Nobody has done a fair fight (properly tuned simple baseline vs AI) for car rebalancing.** This is a strong angle because your project hypothesis is literally "MARL's advantage degrades under shift."
46: 
47: ### Gap 4 — No shared, open "test track" exists
48: For your exact problem (zone-based car rebalancing + the shift test built in):
49: - The only open Gymnasium environments are for **bike-sharing**, not ride-hailing.
50: - The famous **DiDi KDD Cup 2020** dataset is real, but not freely released.
51: 
52: **Building and releasing your own seeded, reproducible environment is itself a contribution** — people love an open benchmark.
53: 
54: ### Gap 5 — Nobody asked "which part of the goal breaks first?"
55: Your AI's reward mixes three goals:
56: ```
57: Reward = − α·waiting time − β·empty driving + γ·served rides
58: ```
59: 
60: If performance drops when demand shifts — **which of the three is the culprit?**
61: 
62: - Did the AI stop serving rides? (γ)
63: - Did it panic-reposition and waste empty km? (β)
64: - Did waiting times explode? (α)
65: 
66: Some recent work used adaptive reward weights (maritime containers) and human-preference rewards (RLHF, 2026) — but **nobody used "turn off each reward part and re-test under shift" as a diagnostic.** That answer is your "why", and it turns a negative result into a full paper.
67: 
68: ---
69: 
70: ## 2. Things you can do differently (options table)
71: 
72: | # | Idea | What's different | Effort |
73: |---|---|---|---|
74: | 1 | **Run BOTH IPPO and MAPPO**, show their separate degradation curves | Tests Gap 2 — which one overfits training | Low |
75: | 2 | **Tune your baselines properly** (like you'd tune the AI) | Tests Gap 3 — fair fight; might reveal AI's gain was fake | Low |
76: | 3 | **Watch performance over time**, not just final averages | Tests Gap 1 — Fujimoto's time-series method | Medium |
77: | 4 | **Turn off each reward term one at a time** under shift | Tests Gap 5 — finds the weak link | Low |
78: | 5 | **Train with random demand patterns** (domain randomization) | One mitigation fix to compare against "no fix" | Low–Medium |
79: | 6 | Feed the AI **"what regime is this?"** info (morning peak, weekend, event…) | Inspired by a 2026 regime-conditioned repositioning paper | Medium |
80: | 7 | **Adversarial training** (prepare for worst-case demand) | State-of-the-art fix (PD-RSAC, 2026) — cite as future work | High |
81: | 8 | **Share parameters between agents** | MAPPO paper confirms it trains faster | Low |
82: | 9 | **Release your environment + protocol publicly** | Reproducibility is an explicit program value | Low |
83: 
84: > Tip: Dynamic zones (re-drawing district borders as demand changes) is possible but risky — only try it if the core project finishes early.
85:

93: | Bonus: reward-term ablation (which goal breaks first?) | Gap 5 |

97: That's a full, publishable story **without inventing anything new** — exactly what the program brief wants.

1: # PS2 — Project Plan (what we are actually doing)
2: 
3: **Working title:** How robust are independent (IPPO) and centralized-training (MAPPO)
4: MARL approaches for dynamic fleet rebalancing under passenger-demand distribution shift?
5: 
6: This document turns the advisor discussion into one concrete picture: what the research
7: is, what is already done, what needs to be done, and in what order.
8: 
9: ---
10: 
11: ## 1. The shape of the project (agreed with sir)
12: 
13: ```
14: Main problem:      demand distribution shift        <- the research question lives here
15: Main comparison:   IPPO vs MAPPO                    <- our instrument, NOT the project itself
16: Reference point:   one strong, tuned non-RL baseline<- makes results believable
17: Optional extras:   reward-part check, released benchmark env   <- only if time allows
18: ```
19: 
20: ### Pillar A — Demand distribution shift (the problem)
21: When we train the zone agents, they see some demand pattern (which zones are busy,
22: at what times). At deployment time that pattern may be different. We study what
23: happens to a frozen, trained policy when this happens.
24: 
25: - How much does performance drop as demand moves away from training?
26: - Which kind of shift hurts most?
27: - Does the policy recover over time, or stay broken?
28: 
29: ### Pillar B — IPPO vs MAPPO (the comparison)
30: Both are standard choices in MARL rebalancing papers. They differ in one thing:
31: - **IPPO:** every zone agent learns completely alone.
32: - **MAPPO:** during training a shared helper sees everything (central critic);
33:   agents still act alone at deployment.
34: 
35: Hypothesis to TEST (not assume): *MAPPO memorizes the training demand more because of
36: its shared helper, so it degrades faster under shift.* Existing evidence goes both
37: ways — POMAC (Hu et al.) says centralized critics can overfit; but the SAF paper's OOD
38: test found MAPPO MORE robust than IPPO on coordination shifts. Exactly why it must be
39: tested in our setting.
40: 
41: ### Pillar C — Stronger baseline (the reference point)
42: Most papers compare their AI against a lazy/random rule. RLSCALE-BENCH showed that a
43: *properly tuned* simple rule can beat all six DRL methods on cost (autoscaling domain).
44: We build/tune at least ONE genuinely competitive non-RL rebalancing strategy and ask:
45: does MARL still add value as demand drifts away from training?
46: 
47: ---
48: 
49: ## 2. Research questions (final form)
50: 
51: | # | Question | Answered by |
52: |---|---|---|
53: | RQ1 | How much does performance degrade as demand moves away from the training distribution? | Degradation curves per shift scenario |
54: | RQ2 | Do IPPO and MAPPO degrade differently? | Head-to-head curves, same seeds, same scenarios |
55: | RQ3 | Does MARL still beat a well-tuned baseline under shift — on which scenarios does the baseline break instead? | Tuned-baseline overlay on the same plots |
56: 
57: ---
58: 
59: ## 3. What "demand distribution shift" means for us (to finalize)
60: 
61: | Type | Meaning | Example knob in simulator |
62: |---|---|---|
63: | Spatial | Hotspots move to different zones | Swap busy-zone weights between regions |
64: | Temporal | Peak hours move (earlier/later/longer) | Shift rush-hour window by ±2h |
65: | Magnitude | Same pattern, different volume | Multiply request rates ×0.5 / ×2 |
66: | New hotspot | Event appears where none existed in training | Inject an event burst in a dead zone |
67: | Event-like | Sudden short spike anywhere | Random 30-min surge |
68: 
69: **Suggested priority:** magnitude + spatial first (easiest to control and explain),
70: then event spike, temporal if time permits. Training always happens ONLY on the base
71: distribution; each scenario is a separate test config.
72: 
73: ---
74: 
75: ## 4. Literature status (already checked — do not redo)
76: 
77: Written up in `notesv2.md` (8 anchor papers verified). Quick map:
78: 
79: - Fujimoto 2024 = our testing method (train -> freeze -> shift -> watch over time). Never applied to vehicles.
80: - MAPPO / IPPO / POMAC = our two algorithms + the overfitting story. Only tested on games.
81: - RLSCALE-BENCH = proof tuned baselines can beat DRL (autoscaling, not fleets).
82: - Lee et al. 2024 = closest fleet paper, but supply disruption (train lines), MADDPG.
83: - PD-RSAC 2026 = robust-by-design fix for EV dispatch; **single-agent RL**, NOT MARL. A fix, not a measurement.
84: - RideGym / CoRide exist, but none is zone-rebalancing + built-in shift protocol.
85: 
86: Extra search already done (the exact thing sir asked us to verify):
87: 
88: > **Did anyone compare CTDE vs independent-learning MARL from an OOD/generalization view?**
89: > No direct head-to-head exists. Closest:
90: > - Mahajan et al., arXiv:2202.00104 — generalization under TEAM COMPOSITION change
91: >   (QMIX/VDN/IPPO/MAPPO evaluated), but shift = teammate capabilities, not demand.
92: > - Liu et al. (SAF), arXiv:2210.03022 — OOD test on coordination/heterogeneity levels;
93: >   MAPPO held up better than IPPO there. Side result, not their focus.
94: > - Fujimoto 2024 — OOD evaluation methodology, decentralized PPO only, no comparison.
95: 
96: **Wording rule (sir's warning):** never claim "nobody studied demand changes in fleets."
97: Robust/predictive rebalancing and distributionally robust RL exist. Our claim is
98: narrower: nobody ran the clean train-on-A / deploy-on-B measurement for zone-level
99: rebalancing MARL with IPPO vs MAPPO plus a tuned baseline.
100: 
101: Still open (transport side):
102: - [ ] Survey classical rebalancing families: demand-supply imbalance rules,
103:       optimization-based (LP/min-cost flow), predictive (MPC-style)
104: - [ ] Pick 1–2 most dangerous ones to tune hard against our MARL
105: - [ ] Check FleetPy properly (what it is, does it fit our 5 requirements)
106: 
107: ---
108: 
109: ## 5. Simulator decision (compare before committing)
110: 
111: Sir's five requirements — whatever we pick must do all of these well:
112: 
113: 1. Control the passenger-demand distribution
114: 2. Easily create spatial/temporal/magnitude shifts
115: 3. Control fleet rebalancing directly
116: 4. Integrate IPPO/MAPPO cleanly
117: 5. Run many experiments quickly
118: 
119: | Option | Strengths | Risks |
120: |---|---|---|
121: | RideGym | Gymnasium-ready, real ride-hailing mechanics | Not built for zone rebalancing; shift injection awkward; unknown speed |
122: | FleetPy | Purpose-built fleet simulator, used by several papers | Heavy learning curve; MARL hooks unverified |
123: | Custom PettingZoo/Gymnasium zone env (9–20 zones) | Total control of demand/shifts/metrics; fastest runs; simplest | We must build & validate it (but that IS contribution #4) |
124: 
125: Lean (per sir): custom zone-based env is likely cleanest.
126: **Decision due end of Week 3 after a 1-day RideGym/FleetPy spike.**
127: 
128: ---
129: 
130: ## 6. Baseline candidates (Pillar C details)
131: 
132: Sanity floor only (not the point of the project): random repositioning, do-nothing.
133: 
134: Real candidates to investigate, then tune hard:
135: 
136: | # | Method | Idea in one line | Tuning knobs |
137: |---|---|---|---|
138: | B1 | Greedy demand-following | Every T minutes, send idle cars toward zones where predicted demand minus supply is highest | horizon T, move fraction, trigger threshold |
139: | B2 | Proportional / inventory rule | Keep each zone's idle cars proportional to its expected share of demand | target ratios, restock rate |
140: | B3 | Optimization-based rebalancing | Each step solve a small min-cost flow / LP: "move X cars from A to B to match predicted OD demand" | prediction source, cost weights, solver time limit |
141: | B4 | Predictive look-ahead (MPC-style) | Plan repositioning for the next few hours from forecast demand; execute only now | forecast horizon, replan interval |
142: 
143: **Fair-fight rule:** the baseline gets the SAME tuning budget as the RL runs
144: (grid search over its knobs on the training distribution), and it may use the same
145: demand forecasts the RL agents see. Otherwise the comparison means nothing.
146: 
147: Deliverable: pick B3 or B4 as "the strong baseline" + keep B1 as the classic
148: literature baseline. Tune all of them.
149: 
150: ---
151: 
152: ## 7. Metrics & experiment design
153: 
154: Metrics (logged every episode, before AND after shift):
155: - Served request rate (%)
156: - Average passenger waiting time
157: - Empty kilometers / wasted repositioning
158: - (optional) fairness across zones, profit
159: 
160: Protocol (Fujimoto-style):
161: 1. Train IPPO and MAPPO on base distribution, same seeds (>= 5), same budget,
162:    hyperparameters tuned fairly for each.
163: 2. Freeze policies. Evaluate on: in-distribution test + each shift scenario.
164: 3. Watch performance over TIME after the shift hits (not one final number).
165: 4. Overlay tuned baselines on identical scenarios — no training needed.
166: 5. Report degradation curves: performance vs shift strength (e.g., magnitude x0.5, x0.75, x1.25, x1.5).
167: 
168: Plots we want at the end:
169: - Degradation curves per method per shift type
170: - IPPO vs MAPPO head-to-head under strongest shift
171: - MARL vs tuned-baseline gap vs shift distance
172:

215: > **Method:** custom zone-based simulator with controlled demand shifts; train IPPO and

1: # PS2 — Literature Notes (what I read, and the holes I found)
2: 
3: Project: MARL for dynamic fleet rebalancing. The one question I keep coming back to:
4: does the AI still work when passenger demand at deployment looks different from the
5: demand it was trained on?
6: 
7: These are my  notes from going through the literature (mostly via web search it is posible i might have missed some things )
8: 
9: ---
10: 
11: ## Things I read (with links)
12: 
13: **1. Fujimoto, Suetterlein, Chatterjee & Ganguly (2024)** — "Assessing the Impact of
14: Distribution Shift on Reinforcement Learning Performance"
15: [arXiv:2402.03590](https://arxiv.org/abs/2402.03590)
16: 
17: This is the paper that gave me the whole framing. The idea: everyone checks how well
18: an AI does on the same situations it was trained on, and that hides the fact that the
19: AI might have just memorized the training instead of learning something general. They
20: say you should stress-test a *trained* AI by changing the situation after training,
21: and watch its score over time instead of just looking at one final number.
22: 
23: The hole in it: they show this idea works on video games (Atari) and a power-grid
24: toy problem. Not on cars, fleets, or passenger demand. So the *way of testing* is
25: exactly what I need, but nobody has used it for ride-hailing rebalancing.
26: 
27: **2. Yu, Velu, Vinitsky, Gao, Wang, Bayen & Wu (2021/2022)** — "The Surprising
28: Effectiveness of PPO in Cooperative, Multi-Agent Games"
29: [arXiv:2103.01955](https://arxiv.org/abs/2103.01955)
30: 
31: This is the MAPPO paper. MAPPO is the version where, during training, each agent gets
32: a shared helper that sees everything (called a central critic), but at the end each
33: agent makes decisions on its own. The paper compares this against IPPO (where every
34: agent trains completely alone) across StarCraft, Hanabi, Google Research Football,
35: and particle environments.
36: 
37: The hole in it: it's all video games. There's no idea of demand, no change after
38: training, and nothing about whether these AIs keep working when the world changes.
39: It tells me *how* to run MAPPO and IPPO, but nothing about which one breaks less when
40: demand shifts.
41: 
42: **3. Schroeder de Witt, Gupta, Makoviychuk, Torr, Sun & Whiteson (2020)** — "Is
43: Independent Learning All You Need in the StarCraft Multi-Agent Challenge?"
44: [arXiv:2011.09533](https://arxiv.org/abs/2011.09533)
45: 
46: This is the IPPO paper. It shows something surprisingly simple: even if each agent
47: ignores the other agents entirely and just learns its own way of deciding, it does as
48: well as (or better than) the fancy methods that try to make agents work together.
49: 
50: The hole in it: again a video game (StarCraft), and the "change" it deals with is
51: other agents changing *during training*, not the demand changing *after* training.
52: 
53: **4. Hu, Hu & Liao (2021)** — "Policy Regularization via Noisy Advantage Values for
54: Cooperative Multi-agent Actor-Critic methods"
55: [arXiv:2106.14334](https://arxiv.org/abs/2106.14334)
56: 
57: This is the paper that names the exact failure mode I care about. They call it
58: **POMAC** — the policies overfitting in multi-agent cooperation. Their story: when
59: agents train together, the numbers they use to decide whether an action was good or
60: bad come from a small sample and are a bit wrong. This wrongness pushes the agents'
61: behavior in a bad direction and stops them from trying new things. They fix it by
62: adding random noise to those numbers.
63: 
64: The hole in it: POMAC is described as a problem that happens *while training*, and
65: they test the fix on StarCraft. Nobody has connected this "memorizing too much" idea
66: to *demand changing after training* — i.e., whether MAPPO's shared helper makes the
67: AI memorize the training demand and therefore fall apart faster when demand changes.
68: That connection is my hypothesis to test.
69: 
70: **5. Zhang, Sun, Zhao, Chu, Sarkani & Fossaceca (2026)** — "When Does Deep RL Beat
71: Calibrated Baselines? A Benchmark Study on Adaptive Resource Control"
72: (RLSCALE-BENCH) [arXiv:2605.26418](https://arxiv.org/abs/2605.26418)
73: 
74: This is the paper that scared me a little, in a good way. They built a benchmark for
75: cloud-server autoscaling and compared six deep-learning AI methods against a *properly
76: tuned* simple rule (the real Kubernetes autoscaler, set the way a company would
77: actually set it). Findings:
78: - the properly tuned simple rule had the lowest cost on all six workloads and never
79:   broke a service rule on steady traffic,
80: - it only fell behind the AI on sudden, jumpy workloads (and there, the AI cut rule
81:   breaks by 54%, but cost 24% more),
82: - and no single method wins everywhere — the winner changed by up to four places
83:   when conditions changed.
84: 
85: The hole in it: it's about cloud servers, not cars. But the lesson transfers straight
86: to me: most fleet papers compare their AI against a lazy/random simple method, so
87: "AI beats the simple method" might just mean the simple method was bad. Nobody has
88: done this fair, properly-tuned comparison for ride-hailing rebalancing under demand
89: change.
90: 
91: **6. Donatus, Ter, Ajayi & Udekwe (2025)** — "Multi-Agent Reinforcement Learning in
92: Intelligent Transportation Systems: A Comprehensive Survey"
93: [arXiv:2508.20315](https://arxiv.org/abs/2508.20315)
94: 
95: This is my survey anchor. It reviews MARL across traffic lights, connected cars,
96: logistics, and ride-hailing. It lists the big open problems: scaling, things changing
97: while learning, dividing credit fairly between agents, communication limits, and the
98: gap between simulation and the real world.
99: 
100: The hole in it: it's a survey, so by definition it just summarizes what's out there.
101: But it confirms that the specific question I care about — how rebalancing AIs behave
102: when the demand pattern changes between training and real use — isn't treated as a
103: main topic anywhere in it.
104: 
105: **7. Lee, Slama & Leclercq (2024)** — "Assessing How Ride-hailing Rebalancing
106: Strategies Improve the Resilience of Multi-modal Transportation Systems"
107: [arXiv:2412.00276](https://arxiv.org/abs/2412.00276)
108: 
109: This is the closest paper to mine that I found, and I need to be honest about that.
110: They study ride-hailing rebalancing with a MARL approach (MADDPG) during disruptions —
111: specifically **broken train lines**. Riders can switch transport modes, demand
112: forecasts are uncertain, and they measure how well the system holds up during the
113: disruption.
114: 
115: The hole in it (and this is the key difference): their disruption is a *supply*
116: problem — the train network fails, so demand for cars suddenly jumps. They are not
117: testing how the AI behaves when the *demand pattern itself* is different from
118: training. Also they use MADDPG, not the PPO family. Different question.
119: 
120: **8. Nguyen, Nguyen, Le, Pham, Do & El Ghaoui (2026)** — "A Distributionally Robust
121: Reinforcement Learning Framework for Constrained Urban EV Dispatch" (PD-RSAC)
122: [arXiv:2604.25848](https://arxiv.org/abs/2604.25848)
123: 
124: A 2026 paper on electric ride-hailing dispatch that explicitly tries to handle demand
125: change. They train their AI to be safe against a whole *family* of possible shifted
126: demand patterns, and they force every action to be physically possible with a math
127: solver. Results on a simulated EV fleet built from New York taxi data.
128: 
129: The hole in it: it's a *fix*, not a *measurement*. They offer a way to train a more
130: careful AI and show it does well. But they don't run the clean experiment I want:
131: train a normal AI on one demand pattern, put it in a different one, and measure how
132: fast it breaks.
133: 
134: **Also found while looking:** a few open ride-hailing environments exist —
135: **RideGym** ([GitHub](https://github.com/RS2002/RideGym), PyPI) for ride-pooling and
136: order dispatching, and the **CoRide** simulator (CIKM 2019). There are also
137: bike-sharing environments (iREbikeLANCE, Bike3S, BSSLib). So the old claim "no open
138: ride-hailing environments exist" is wrong — but none of them is built for zone-based
139: rebalancing with the "train here, test over there" setup.
140: 
141: ---
142: 
143: ## General summary
144: 
145: The papers I read fall into three groups:
146: - **How to test for robustness** (Fujimoto) — the method I want to use, but never
147:   applied to cars.
148: - **How to train the agents** (MAPPO, IPPO, POMAC) — the algorithms I want to
149:   compare, but only tested on video games.
150: - **Close fleet work** (Lee, PD-RSAC, RLSCALE-BENCH, the survey) — closest to my
151:   topic, but each stops just short: supply disruptions instead of demand change,
152:   a fix instead of a measurement, a different domain, or just a summary.
153: 
154: So I have the testing method, the algorithms, and the domain — but nobody has put
155: them together the way I plan to.
156: 
157: ---
158: 
159: ## What's missing (in short)
160: 
161: - **1.** The exact test (train on one demand pattern, test on others) hasn't been
162:   run on cars. Closest work: Fujimoto (games/grids), Lee (supply disruptions),
163:   some cross-day/city transfer. Nobody did zone rebalancing + demand change +
164:   IPPO/MAPPO + multiple runs.
165: - **2.** Nobody compared IPPO vs MAPPO under demand change in fleets.
166: - **3.** Nobody did a fair fight with a well-tuned simple method for rebalancing —
167:   only for autoscaling (RLSCALE-BENCH).
168: - **4.** No open test track for zone-rebalancing with the demand-change setup built
169:   in. (RideGym etc. exist, but for dispatch/pooling, not this.)
170: - **5.** Nobody turned off one score part at a time and re-tested under demand
171:   change, to see which goal breaks first.
172: 
173: ## The gaps at a glance
174: 
175: | Gap | Honest status after checking |
176: |---|---|
177: | 1. The exact demand-change test on cars | Mostly true — pieces exist (Fujimoto, Lee, cross-day transfer), but no one did *this* setup |
178: | 2. IPPO vs MAPPO under demand change | True — no paper compares them under demand change in fleets |
179: | 3. Fair, well-tuned simple method for rebalancing | True for fleets — only done for autoscaling (RLSCALE-BENCH) |
180: | 4. Open test track | Partly — RideGym etc. exist, but nothing for zone-rebalancing + demand-change |
181: | 5. Turning off one score part at a time under change | True — score shaping exists, but not this check |
